Her husband and domino friends eventually urged her to see a doctor when they noticed her clothes hanging loosely and her reluctance to eat at social events, and after her primary care physician expressed concern about her low weight and asked about her preoccupation with staying \"small\" in this new phase of life, she was referred for psychiatric assessment.\n- Chief Complaint: “Lately all I can think about is not getting bigger as I get older, and I judge myself by what the scale says more than anything else.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S043 (Restrictive_Intake): Most days now I just pick at a little yogurt or a few crackers and tell my husband I already ate at work, and I’ve cut out rice, pasteles, and anything with oil because in my head those foods feel like they’ll go straight to my belly and make me huge for retirement. \n- S044 (Low_Weight): My uniforms hang off me and my wedding ring spins on my finger, but when my doctor or my sisters say I’m too thin, I shrug it off and think, ‘No, I still have a little pouch here, I should probably lose a bit more before I stop.’\n- S045 (Fear_of_Weight_Gain): If I even think about eating a full plate of food, especially things like pan sobao or pernil at a family party, I get this tight feeling in my chest and picture myself turning into one of those “chubby old ladies,” so I’d rather drink coffee or water and keep my stomach empty. \n- S046 (Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance): Every morning I weigh myself before work, and if the number is even half a pound higher I feel like a failure and spend the whole day replaying every bite I ate, like it proves I’m weak and not ready for this next stage of my life.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: I’ve started turning down extra shifts and skipping our usual church potlucks and domino nights because I get so anxious about the food, and at home my husband and I argue more because he says he doesn’t recognize me and     "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see us today?\n\nPatient: My primary doctor referred me here, though I'm a little nervous to talk about it. Lately all I can think about is not getting bigger as I get older, and I judge myself by what the scale says more than anything else.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself restricting the amount of food you eat or engaging in excessive exercise in an attempt to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Yes, I restrict my food quite a bit, usually just picking at some yogurt or crackers and telling my husband I already ate at work. I've completely cut out rice, pasteles, and oil because I'm terrified they will make me gain weight, though I don't really do any excessive exercise.\n\nDoctor: Do you often check your body shape or weight, such as frequently weighing yourself or looking in the mirror to check for any signs of weight gain or fatness?\n\nPatient: Yes, every single morning before work I step on the scale. If the number goes up by even half a pound, I feel like a complete failure and spend the whole day obsessing over every bite I ate, like it proves I'm too weak for this next stage of my life.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your menstrual cycle, such as irregular periods or a complete stop in your menstrual bleeding, since you started restricting your food intake?\n\nPatient: Oh, well, I am 64 years old and went through menopause quite a few years ago. Because of that, I don't get periods anymore anyway.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me at what age you started having these concerns about your weight and body shape, and have they been persistent over the years or have they worsened recently?\n\nPatient: These concerns actually started very recently, as I've been preparing to retire at age 64. I never had these worries when I was younger, but they've gotten much worse lately with the stress of leaving my job. I just want to stay healthy and avoid becoming a burden on my family as I get older.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that these concerns started recently with the stress of retirement, but can you tell me if there are any specific thoughts or fears about your body shape or weight that are driving your desire to restrict food and maintain a certain weight?\n\nPatient: I am terrified of turning into a \"chubby old lady\" as I age, and just thinking about eating a full plate of food like pan sobao or pernil makes my chest feel so tight that I'd rather keep my stomach empty. Also, when I weigh myself every morning, if the number goes up even half a pound, I feel like a weak failure who isn't ready for this next stage of my life.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight and becoming a 'chubby old lady' is related to a fear of losing control or a fear of how others might perceive you, or is it more about a specific concern with the appearance of your body?\n\nPatient: It honestly feels like a mix of both. Gaining weight feels like a sign of weakness and a loss of control, especially now that I am retiring and losing my daily routine. But at the same time, I am terrified of the physical change and seeing my body turn into a chubby old lady as I get older.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your fear of becoming a 'chubby old lady' affects your daily life and relationships, beyond just your eating habits and weight checking?"

    "content": "Previous Candidates:\n[\"F50.0\"]\n\nInterview Transcript:\n\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see us today?\n\nPatient: My primary doctor referred me here, though I'm a little nervous to talk about it. Lately all I can think about is not getting bigger as I get older, and I judge myself by what the scale says more than anything else.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself restricting the amount of food you eat or engaging in excessive exercise in an attempt to control your weight?\n\nPatient: Yes, I restrict my food quite a bit, usually just picking at some yogurt or crackers and telling my husband I already ate at work. I've completely cut out rice, pasteles, and oil because I'm terrified they will make me gain weight, though I don't really do any excessive exercise.\n\nDoctor: Do you often check your body shape or weight, such as frequently weighing yourself or looking in the mirror to check for any signs of weight gain or fatness?\n\nPatient: Yes, every single morning before work I step on the scale. If the number goes up by even half a pound, I feel like a complete failure and spend the whole day obsessing over every bite I ate, like it proves I'm too weak for this next stage of my life.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your menstrual cycle, such as irregular periods or a complete stop in your menstrual bleeding, since you started restricting your food intake?\n\nPatient: Oh, well, I am 64 years old and went through menopause quite a few years ago. Because of that, I don't get periods anymore anyway.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me at what age you started having these concerns about your weight and body shape, and have they been persistent over the years or have they worsened recently?\n\nPatient: These concerns actually started very recently, as I've been preparing to retire at age 64. I never had these worries when I was younger, but they've gotten much worse lately with the stress of leaving my job. I just want to stay healthy and avoid becoming a burden on my family as I get older.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that these concerns started recently with the stress of retirement, but can you tell me if there are any specific thoughts or fears about your body shape or weight that are driving your desire to restrict food and maintain a certain weight?\n\nPatient: I am terrified of turning into a \"chubby old lady\" as I age, and just thinking about eating a full plate of food like pan sobao or pernil makes my chest feel so tight that I'd rather keep my stomach empty. Also, when I weigh myself every morning, if the number goes up even half a pound, I feel like a weak failure who isn't ready for this next stage of my life.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight and becoming a 'chubby old lady' is related to a fear of losing control or a fear of how others might perceive you, or is it more about a specific concern with the appearance of your body?\n\nPatient: It honestly feels like a mix of both. Gaining weight feels like a sign of weakness and a loss of control, especially now that I am retiring and losing my daily routine. But at the same time, I am terrified of the physical change and seeing my body turn into a chubby old lady as I get older.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your fear of becoming a 'chubby old lady' affects your daily life and relationships, beyond just your eating habits and weight checking?\n\nPatient: It has really isolated me because I've started turning down extra work shifts and skipping our church potlucks and domino nights since the anxiety around food is just too much. At home, my husband and I argue constantly because he says he doesn't recognize me anymore and worries I'm disappearing right in front of him.\n\nDoctor: When you think about becoming a 'chubby old lady,' are you more concerned about the potential health problems associated with weight gain or the aesthetic and social implications of having a larger body?\n\nPatient: It actually started as a health concern when my doctor mentioned my borderline blood pressure and cholesterol, and I wanted to avoid being a burden. But now, it is much more about the fear of looking chubby and what that represents. Gaining weight feels like a personal failure and a sign of weakness, making me feel completely unprepared for this next stage of my life rather than just worrying about health problems.\n\nDoctor: When you imagine yourself as a 'chubby old lady,' what is the most distressing part of that image to you: the potential health consequences or the way you would look and feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: The most distressing part is definitely how I would look and feel about myself, rather than any health issues. To me, that image represents a complete loss of control and weakness, and gaining even a little weight makes me feel like a failure who isn't ready for retirement. I've ended up judging my entire worth by what the scale says every morning because of that fear."

But at the same time, I am terrified of the physical change and seeing my body turn into a chubby old lady as I get older.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your fear of becoming a 'chubby old lady' affects your daily life and relationships, beyond just your eating habits and weight checking?\n\nPatient: It has really isolated me because I've started turning down extra work shifts and skipping our church potlucks and domino nights since the anxiety around food is just too much. At home, my husband and I argue constantly because he says he doesn't recognize me anymore and worries I'm disappearing right in front of him.\n\nDoctor: When you think about becoming a 'chubby old lady,' are you more concerned about the potential health problems associated with weight gain or the aesthetic and social implications of having a larger body?\n\nPatient: It actually started as a health concern when my doctor mentioned my borderline blood pressure and cholesterol, and I wanted to avoid being a burden. But now, it is much more about the fear of looking chubby and what that represents. Gaining weight feels like a personal failure and a sign of weakness, making me feel completely unprepared for this next stage of my life rather than just worrying about health problems.\n\nDoctor: When you imagine yourself as a 'chubby old lady,' what is the most distressing part of that image to you: the potential health consequences or the way you would look and feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: The most distressing part is definitely how I would look and feel about myself, rather than any health issues. To me, that image represents a complete loss of control and weakness, and gaining even a little weight makes me feel like a failure who isn't ready for retirement. I've ended up judging my entire worth by what the scale says every morning because of that fear.\n\nDoctor: When you experience anxiety or distress about your weight, is it primarily because you fear the health consequences of being overweight, or is it more about the emotional distress and self-criticism you feel when you perceive yourself as not meeting your ideal body shape or weight?\n\nPatient: It is almost entirely about the emotional distress and self-criticism now, rather than physical health. My mood is completely dictated by the scale every morning, and even a half-pound increase makes me feel incredibly weak, self-critical, and unprepared for retirement. It started with my doctor's warning about my blood pressure, but now it is just about feeling like a failure if I can't stay small."
